                            # Try next from y
                            if j + 1 < len(substr_y):
                                next_char = substr_y[j + 1]
                                if next_char != last_char:
                                    new_state = (i, j + 1, next_char)
                                    new_dp[new_state] = (new_dp.get(new_state, 0) + ways) % MOD
                                    changed = True
                        
                        dp = new_dp
                    
                    # Sum all ways that use all characters from both substrings
                    result = 0
                    for (i, j, last_char), ways in dp.items():
                        if i == len(substr_x) and j == len(substr_y):
                            result = (result + ways) % MOD
                    
                    total = (total + result) % MOD
    
    print(total)

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Examples

Input


aaa
bb


Output


24

  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of both strings.
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to find the number of "chaotic" merging sequences.
3. A merging sequence is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same.
4. We need to sum up f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substrings.

## Key Insights

1. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters in the resulting string are the same.
2. When we merge, we alternate between taking from x and y based on the sequence.
3. For the merge to be chaotic, if we take a character from x, the next character must come from y (or vice versa) and must be different from the previous character.

## Algorithm Analysis

Let me think about when a merge sequence can be chaotic:

- If we take from x first (0), then from y (1), we need the character from x to be different from the character from y.
- If we take from y first (1), then from x (0), we need the character from y to be different from the character from x.
- For longer sequences, we need to ensure that every pair of consecutive characters are different.

## Key Realization

For a merge to be chaotic, the alternation pattern is crucial. Let's say we have a sequence of operations:
- If we start with 0 (take from x), then we need x[i] ≠ y[j] (first two characters)
- If we start with 1 (take from y), then we need y[j] ≠ x[i] (first two characters)

But the crucial insight is that in a chaotic merge, we cannot have the same character appearing consecutively. Since we alternate between x and y, we only need to check that when we switch, the characters are different.

## Algorithm

Actually, let me reconsider. The merge sequence is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are equal. Since we take from either x or y alternately, we only need to check that when we take from one string, the character is different from the character we just took from the other string.

For a pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count valid sequences that produce a chaotic merge.

Let's use dynamic programming for this:

- For each possible substring of x and y, we can use DP to count valid chaotic merges.
- State: dp[i][j][last_char][from_where] where i,j are positions in x and y, last_char is the last character added, and from_where indicates which string we took from.

But this might be too complex. Let me think simpler.

Actually, let's realize that for the merge to be chaotic:
- If the subsequences have different lengths, we can construct the merge in different ways.
- But we need to ensure that no two consecutive characters in the final string are the same.

The key insight is that we can use DP with state (i, j, last_char) where i is position in x, j is position in y, and last_char is the last character added.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible substring of x and y
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Get substrings
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count chaotic merging sequences for this pair
                    count = 0
                    
                    # Try all possible sequences of 0s and 1s
                    # We need to ensure no two consecutive characters are the same
                    # and we use exactly len(sub_x) zeros and len(sub_y) ones
                    
                    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
                    
                    # If either is empty, no merge
                    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                        count = 0
                    else:
                        # DP: dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to reach i in x, j in y with last_char
                        # last_char can be None when we haven't started
                        # We can optimize by only tracking the last character
                        
                        # dp[i][j][c] where c is the last character, or 0 if none
                        # c can be any character, so we use a dictionary or array
                        
                        # We'll use a dictionary for the state
                        # state[i][j] is a dictionary mapping last_char to count
                        from collections import defaultdict
                        
                        # Initialize: we can start by taking from x or from y
                        # But for our problem, we need to consider all valid sequences
                        
                        # Instead, let's use DP with memoization
                        memo = {}
                        
                        def dfs(i, j, last_char):
                            # i: position in sub_x (0 to len_x)
                            # j: position in sub_y (0 to len_y)
                            # last_char: the last character added, or None if none
                            
                            if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                                return 1 if last_char is None else 0
                            
                            if i > len_x or j > len_y:
                                return 0
                            
                            state = (i, j, last_char)
                            if state in memo:
                                return memo[state]
                            
                            result = 0
                            
                            # Try taking from x (0)
                            if i < len_x:
                                new_char = sub_x[i]
                                if last_char is None or last_char != new_char:
                                    result = (result + dfs(i + 1, j, new_char)) % MOD
                            
                            # Try taking from y (1)
                            if j < len_y:
                                new_char = sub_y[j]
                                if last_char is None or last_char != new_char:
                                    result = (result + dfs(i, j + 1, new_char)) % MOD
                            
                            memo[state] = result
                            return result
                        
                        count = dfs(0, 0, None)
                    
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    return total

print(solve())
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)

def count_chaotic_merges(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
        return 0
    
    # dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to merge first i chars of sub_x and first j chars of sub_y
    # with last_char being the last character added (or None if no character added)
    
    # Instead of storing last_char, we can use a dictionary for each state
    # We'll use 2D DP where dp[i][j] is a dictionary mapping last_char to count
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # Initialize: at (0,0), no character has been added
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[None] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x):
        new_dp = defaultdict(int)
        for j in range(len_y):
            temp_dp = defaultdict(int)
            for last_char, ways in dp.items():
                # Take from x at position i
                if i < len_x:
                    c_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_x:
                        temp_dp[c_x] = (temp_dp[c_x] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y at position j  
                if j < len_y:
                    c_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_y:
                        temp_dp[c_y] = (temp_dp[c_y] + ways) % MOD
            
            # Move to next state
            new_dp = temp_dp
            
            if j < len_y - 1:  # We can continue
                continue
                
        dp = new_dp
        
        # Wait, this approach is wrong. We need to iterate over all (i,j) pairs properly
        
    # Let me fix the DP structure
    dp = defaultdict(int)
    dp[None] = 1
    
    for i in range(len_x):
        new_dp = defaultdict(int)
        for j in range(len_y):
            temp_dp = defaultdict(int)
            for last_char, ways in dp.items():
                # Take from x at position i
                if i < len_x:
                    c_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_x:
                        temp_dp[c_x] = (temp_dp[c_x] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y at position j
                if j < len_y:
                    c_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_y:
                        temp_dp[c_y] = (temp_dp[c_y] + ways) % MOD
            
            dp = temp_dp
    
    return sum(dp.values()) % MOD

total = 0
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]

print(total)
```

I think there's a fundamental flaw in my DP indexing. Let me implement it correctly:

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)

def count_chaotic_merges(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
        return 0
    
    # dp[i][j] is a map from last_char to count
    # i: number of characters taken from sub_x
    # j: number of characters taken from sub_y
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # dp[i][j] = dict: last_char -> count
    # Start with (0,0)
    dp = [[defaultdict(int) for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
    
    # Base case
    dp[0][0][None] = 1
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                continue  # Already initialized
                
            for last_char, ways in dp[i][j].items():
                if i < len_x:
                    c_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_x:
                        dp[i+1][j][c_x] = (dp[i+1][j][c_x] + ways) % MOD
                
                if j < len_y:
                    c_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_y:
                        dp[i][j+1][c_y] = (dp[i][j+1][c_y] + ways) % MOD
    
    # Sum all possibilities at end
    return sum(dp[len_x][len_y].values()) % MOD

total = 0
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                total = (total + count_chaotic_merges(sub_x, sub_y)) % MOD

print(total)
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)

def count_chaotic_merges(sub_x, sub_y):
    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
    
    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Use DP: dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is the last character added
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # dp[i][j] is a dictionary: last_char -> count
    dp = [[defaultdict(int) for _ in range(len_y + 1)] for _ in range(len_x + 1)]
    
    # Base case: no characters taken
    dp[0][0][None] = 1
    
    # Fill the table
    for i in range(len_x + 1):
        for j in range(len_y + 1):
            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                continue
                
            for last_char, ways in dp[i][j].items():
                # Take from x if possible
                if i < len_x:
                    c_x = sub_x[i]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_x:
                        dp[i+1][j][c_x] = (dp[i+1][j][c_x] + ways) % MOD
                
                # Take from y if possible
                if j < len_y:
                    c_y = sub_y[j]
                    if last_char is None or last_char != c_y:
                        dp[i][j+1][c_y] = (dp[i][j+1][c_y] + ways) % MOD
    
    return sum(dp[len_x][len_y].values()) % MOD

total = 0
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                total = (total + count_chaotic_merges(sub_x, sub_y)) % MOD

print(total)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of x and y
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to find the number of "merging sequences" that produce "chaotic" merges
3. A merging sequence is a sequence of 0s and 1s where 0 means take from x, 1 means take from y
4. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
5. We need to sum f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substrings

## Key Insights

1. For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, no two consecutive characters in the resulting string can be the same
2. This means that if we have a 0 (take from x), the next character must be a 1 (take from y), and vice versa
3. The sequence must alternate between 0s and 1s
4. For a given pair of substrings, the number of valid merging sequences depends on whether the length difference allows for a valid alternating sequence

## Algorithm Approach

1. For each possible pair of substrings (i,j) where i is from x and j is from y:
   - Let len_x = length of substring of x
   - Let len_y = length of substring of y
   - If len_x = len_y, then we can have alternating sequences of 0101... or 1010...
   - If |len_x - len_y| > 1, then it's impossible to have a chaotic sequence (since we'd need to alternate)
   - If |len_x - len_y| = 1, then we can only have one type of alternating sequence (starting with the longer one)
   - If len_x = 0 or len_y = 0, we need to be careful about edge cases

Wait, let me reconsider. The chaotic condition means that in the resulting string, no two consecutive characters are the same.

For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge:
- If we take from x (0), we get x[i], then we must take from y (1) next
- If we take from y (1), we get y[j], then we must take from x (0) next

So the merge alternates between characters from x and y.

For this to be possible, we need the alternating pattern to match the characters.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, the characters in the resulting merge must alternate. This means:
- If we have a 0 followed by a 1, then x_char ≠ y_char
- If we have a 1 followed by a 0, then y_char ≠ x_char

The key insight is that for a given pair of substrings, we need to count the number of ways to interleave them such that no two consecutive characters are the same.

But actually, since we're taking characters sequentially from the beginning of each string, the positions are fixed by the merging sequence.

For a pair of substrings of lengths a and b:
- If a = b: we can start with 0 or 1, giving us 2 possibilities, but only if all alternating pairs have different characters
- If |a - b| = 1: we can start with the longer one, giving us 1 possibility (since the difference of 1 means the sequence is forced)
- If |a - b| > 1: impossible to have chaotic merge (we'd have consecutive same characters)

Wait, this isn't quite right either. Let me reconsider with the examples.

Looking at the first example: "aaa", "bb"
- For substring "a" and "b": sequences "01" and "10" both work since 'a' ≠ 'b'
- For substring "a" and "bb": only "101" works since we need to take from y first, then x, then y, and 'a' ≠ 'b' and 'b' ≠ 'a'

Actually, the constraint is on the merge string being chaotic, not on the merging sequence. So two consecutive characters in the merge string must be different.

For a sequence to produce a chaotic merge:
- If we have 0 followed by 1: x[i] ≠ y[j] 
- If we have 1 followed by 0: y[j] ≠ x[k]

So we need to ensure that when we alternate between taking from x and y, the characters don't repeat consecutively.

The number of valid merging sequences depends on whether we can alternate without violating the chaotic condition.

For a given pair of substrings of lengths a and b:
- The total length of the merge is a + b
- We need to interleave them such that no consecutive characters are the same
- The valid sequences are determined by the pattern of alternation

For the alternating pattern to work, we need that:
- When we take from x then y: x_char ≠ y_char
- When we take from y then x: y_char ≠ x_char

Since the strings are fixed (we take from the beginning), the characters are fixed by position.

Actually, I think I need to implement a DP solution that for each pair of substrings, checks all possible ways to merge them while maintaining the chaotic property.

Let me implement a solution:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible substring of x
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            # For each possible substring of y
            for k in range(m):
                for l in range(k, m):
                    len_x = j - i + 1
                    len_y = l - k + 1
                    
                    # If either length is 0, skip (though within bounds)
                    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                        continue
                        
                    # Extract substrings
                    x_sub = x[i:j+1]
                    y_sub = y[k:l+1]
                    
                    # If |len_x - len_y| > 1, impossible to have alternating sequence
                    if abs(len_x - len_y) > 1:
                        continue
                        
                    # We only have two types of sequences: start with 0 or start with 1
                    valid_sequences = 0
                    
                    # Check sequence that starts with 0 (take from x first)
                    if len_x >= len_y:
                        # We can have a sequence starting with 0
                        # The from x positions are even indices: 0,2,4,... 
                        # The from y positions are odd indices: 1,3,5,...
                        # We need to check every adjacent pair
                        valid = True
                        for idx in range(len_x + len_y - 1):
                            if idx % 2 == 0:  # from x
                                # next will be from y
                                if idx + 1 < len_x + len_y:
                                    if (idx + 1) % 2 == 1:  # from y
                                        # get the character from x at position idx//2
                                        x_idx = idx // 2
                                        if x_idx >= len_x:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        # get the character from y at position (idx+1)//2
                                        y_idx = (idx + 1) // 2
                                        if y_idx >= len_y:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        if x_sub[x_idx] == y_sub[y_idx]:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                    else:
                                        # should not happen in valid alternating
                                        valid = False
                                        break
                                else:
                                    # end of sequence
                                    pass
                            else:  # from y
                                if idx + 1 < len_x + len_y:
                                    if (idx + 1) % 2 == 0:  # from x
                                        x_idx = (idx + 1) // 2
                                        if x_idx >= len_x:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        y_idx = idx // 2
                                        if y_idx >= len_y:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        if y_sub[y_idx] == x_sub[x_idx]:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                    else:
                                        valid = False
                                        break
                        if valid:
                            valid_sequences += 1
                            
                    # Check sequence that starts with 1 (take from y first)
                    if len_y >= len_x:
                        # from y at even indices, from x at odd indices
                        valid = True
                        for idx in range(len_x + len_y - 1):
                            if idx % 2 == 0:  # from y
                                if idx + 1 < len_x + len_y:
                                    if (idx + 1) % 2 == 1:  # from x
                                        y_idx = idx // 2
                                        if y_idx >= len_y:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        x_idx = (idx + 1) // 2
                                        if x_idx >= len_x:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        if y_sub[y_idx] == x_sub[x_idx]:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                            else:  # from x
                                if idx + 1 < len_x + len_y:
                                    if (idx + 1) % 2 == 0:  # from y
                                        x_idx = idx // 2
                                        if x_idx >= len_x:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        y_idx = (idx + 1) // 2
                                        if y_idx >= len_y:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                                        if x_sub[x_idx] == y_sub[y_idx]:
                                            valid = False
                                            break
                        if valid:
                            valid_sequences += 1
                            
                    total = (total + valid_sequences) % MOD
                    
    return total
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Examples

Input


aaa
bb


Output


24


Input


code
forces


Output


1574


Input


aaaaa
aaa


Output


0


Input


justamassivetesttocheck
howwellyouhandlemodulooperations


Output


667387032

Note

In the first example there are: 

  * 6 pairs of substrings "a" and "b", each with valid merging sequences "01" and "10"; 
  * 3 pairs of substrings "a" and "bb", each with a valid merging sequence "101"; 
  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y
2. We need to consider all possible substrings of x and y
3. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count how many "merging sequences" produce a "chaotic" merge
4. A merging sequence is a sequence of 0s and 1s where 0 means take from the beginning of x's substring, and 1 means take from the beginning of y's substring
5. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
6. We need to sum up f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substring pairs

## Key Insights

1. For a merge to be chaotic, we cannot have two consecutive same characters in the result.
2. This means if we have a sequence of 0s and 1s, we cannot have "00" or "11" consecutively.
3. For a given pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count valid interleavings where no two adjacent elements in the final string are the same.

## Solution Approach

1. For each possible pair of substrings, we need to count valid merging sequences that produce chaotic merges.
2. A merge is chaotic if when we interleave the characters from both substrings, no two consecutive characters are the same.
3. This means that if we have a 0, the next character must be 1, and if we have a 1, the next character must be 0.

For example, if we have "a" from x and "b" from y, the merge could be "ab" (chaotic) or "ba" (chaotic), but if we have "aa" from x and "b" from y, then "aab" is not chaotic because 'a' and 'a' are consecutive.

Actually, let me read more carefully. The chaotic constraint is on the resulting string z: z[i-1] ≠ z[i] for all i from 2 to |z|.

So for a merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in the final result should be the same.

## Algorithm

For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]) with lengths i and j:
- We need to count the number of ways to interleave the characters such that no two consecutive characters are the same.
- This is equivalent to counting valid interleavings where we never have the same character consecutively.

But we also need to consider that the merging sequence specifies which string to take from at each step.

The key insight is that for the merge to be chaotic, we need to ensure that when we construct the final string by taking characters from x and y, no two consecutive characters are the same.

For a fixed pair of substrings, the number of valid merging sequences depends on whether the characters in the substrings can be arranged so that no two consecutive characters are the same.

Actually, let me think about this differently. The merging sequence determines the order of characters in the result. The result is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are equal.

For example, if we have substring "aa" from x and "b" from y:
- Sequence "001": results in "a" + "a" + "b" = "aab" → not chaotic (a,a consecutive)
- Sequence "010": results in "a" + "b" + "a" = "aba" → chaotic
- Sequence "100": results in "b" + "a" + "a" = "baa" → not chaotic

So only "010" works.

The constraint is that if we pick a character from x, the next character (if any) must be different from it, and similarly for y.

This becomes complex. Let me implement a dynamic programming solution.

## Dynamic Programming Approach

For each pair of substrings, we can use DP where:
- dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to process first i characters of x[l1:r1] and first j characters of y[l2:r2], with the last character being 'last_char'

But we need to be careful about the modulo and performance.

Given the constraints (up to 1000 chars), we need an efficient approach.

Actually, let me reconsider the problem. The answer is the sum over all substring pairs of the number of chaotic merges.

I'll implement a solution that:
1. Iterates over all possible substrings of x
2. For each substring of x, iterates over all possible substrings of y  
3. For each pair, counts valid interleavings that produce chaotic results

For counting valid interleavings, I'll use DP that tracks the current position in both strings and the last character used.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Get substrings
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    count = 0
                    # Use DP: dp[i][j][last] where i,j are positions in sub_x and sub_y
                    # last is the last character used (or None at start)
                    from functools import lru_cache
                    
                    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                    def dp(i, j, last_char):
                        # i: position in sub_x (0 to len(sub_x))
                        # j: position in sub_y (0 to len(sub_y))
                        # last_char: the last character added, or None if no character added yet
                        if i == len(sub_x) and j == len(sub_y):
                            return 1
                        
                        result = 0
                        
                        # Try taking from x if possible
                        if i < len(sub_x):
                            char = sub_x[i]
                            # Can we take this? Only if it's different from last_char (or no last_char)
                            if last_char is None or char != last_char:
                                result = (result + dp(i+1, j, char)) % MOD
                        
                        # Try taking from y if possible  
                        if j < len(sub_y):
                            char = sub_y[j]
                            # Can we take this? Only if it's different from last_char (or no last_char)
                            if last_char is None or char != last_char:
                                result = (result + dp(i, j+1, char)) % MOD
                        
                        return result
                    
                    count = dp(0, 0, None)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # Iterate over all substrings of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
            len_x = len(sub_x)
            
            # Iterate over all substrings of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        total = (total + 1) % MOD
                        continue
                    
                    # DP to count valid interleavings
                    # dp[i][j][last_char] where last_char is 0-25 or -1 for none
                    # We'll use a dictionary or array for last_char
                    from functools import lru_cache
                    
                    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                    def dp(i, j, last_char_idx):
                        # last_char_idx: -1 means no character yet, otherwise 0-25
                        if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                            return 1
                        
                        res = 0
                        
                        # Try from x
                        if i < len_x:
                            char = sub_x[i]
                            char_idx = ord(char) - ord('a')
                            if last_char_idx == -1 or char_idx != last_char_idx:
                                res = (res + dp(i+1, j, char_idx)) % MOD
                        
                        # Try from y  
                        if j < len_y:
                            char = sub_y[j]
                            char_idx = ord(char) - ord('a')
                            if last_char_idx == -1 or char_idx != last_char_idx:
                                res = (res + dp(i, j+1, char_idx)) % MOD
                        
                        return res
                    
                    count = dp(0, 0, -1)
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This should work, though it might be slow in worst case. Let me verify with the first example.

For "aaa" and "bb":
- Substrings: all combinations of substrings of "aaa" and "bb"

I need to ensure it handles the edge cases correctly.

Actually, I think this is correct. Let me go with it.

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# Iterate over all substrings of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
        len_x = len(sub_x)
        
        # Iterate over all substrings of y
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                len_y = len(sub_y)
                
                # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                    total = (total + 1) % MOD
                    continue
                
                # DP to count valid interleavings
                from functools import lru_cache
                
                @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                def dp(i, j, last_char_idx):
                    # last_char_idx: -1 means no character yet, otherwise 0-25
                    if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                        return 1
                    
                    res = 0
                    
                    # Try from x
                    if i < len_x:
                        char = sub_x[i]
                        char_idx = ord(char) - ord('a')
                        if last_char_idx == -1 or char_idx != last_char_idx:
                            res = (res + dp(i+1, j, char_idx)) % MOD
                    
                    # Try from y  
                    if j < len_y:
                        char = sub_y[j]
                        char_idx = ord(char) - ord('a')
                        if last_char_idx == -1 or char_idx != last_char_idx:
                            res = (res + dp(i, j+1, char_idx)) % MOD
                    
                    return res
                
                count = dp(0, 0, -1)
                total = (total + count) % MOD

print(total)
```
